People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is my sleep has gotten really messed up lately. Like, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I should be tired, but then my mind just keeps going and going and I end up staying up half the night. And other times I do fall asleep, but then I wake up way too early, like before I’m supposed to, and I just feel like I never really rested at all.

It’s been getting frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m starting the day normally anymore. I’m either lying there for hours waiting to fall asleep, or I’m awake too early and already exhausted. That’s mostly why I came in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling already there, like before anything has even happened that day. It’s like I open my eyes and I’m already weighed down.

And it’s strange because I’ll be sitting in my own condo — the place I worked really hard to get, and I used to feel proud of it — and instead of feeling comfortable or happy or anything, I just feel like I might cry. Sometimes I look around and think, “This should feel good, I should feel okay here,” but it just doesn’t. It feels kind of hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to it. So… yeah, sad and down, definitely.

I’d say it’s been around for about five months, maybe a little more if I’m being honest. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly terrible, it was more like this fog started settling in and I kept thinking it would lift if I just got through the week, or caught up on things, or stopped overthinking.

Yeah… I have. That’s actually been one of the weirder parts, because it’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… don’t feel anything toward them anymore.

I used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It gave me something to look forward to, and I liked being around people there even if I wasn’t the best dancer or anything. But I just stopped going. At first I told myself I was just skipping one class, then it became another one, and then I kind of stopped even pretending I was going back.

And painting too… I have my paints still sitting out on the table at home. They’ve just been there, like I left them expecting I’d pick them up again, but I’ll look at them and feel nothing. It’s almost like they belong to somebody else, which sounds strange, but that’s how it feels. Like I remember that I used to care about it, but I can’t really access that feeling right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… there were a few stretches where I felt really different, like almost weirdly better. Earlier this month especially, I remember feeling kind of lit up, like I had this energy or confidence that I hadn’t had in a while. Not just “a little less depressed,” but more like I suddenly felt unstoppable, like I could handle everything and maybe I’d been making too big a deal out of how bad I’d been feeling.

But it didn’t exactly feel peaceful, if that makes sense. It was like I was turned up too high. And then little things would set me off. My mom would ask me some normal question, like if I’d eaten or if I’d called someone back, and I’d snap at her like she was criticizing me. Or at work, my supervisor would ask a small follow-up question about something, nothing major, and I’d feel this flash of irritation, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I don’t think that would bother me that much.

So yes, I guess there have been times I felt significantly better, but not in a calm, steady way. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.

I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.

Normally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.

Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.

Like with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.

It wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”

It started feeling noticeable once the mortgage became real, like after the condo stopped being this abstract “I’m buying a place” thing and became… this huge permanent responsibility. Since then it’s felt more like I swing between getting this burst of energy or confidence, and then dropping back into this heavy crash. Before that, I think I was mostly just down. But the noticeable ups and downs have been about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't need to sleep, or where you felt like you could go without sleep for an extended time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.\n\nNormally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see when you were feeling really up or charged?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.

I’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.

Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. And then somehow that would turn into side-job ideas, like setting up bookkeeping work on the side, figuring out what I’d charge, what kind of clients I could get, making lists of business names or little plans.

It’s like after work, when normally I should be tired, I’d get this burst of energy and keep stacking things on. Cleaning, lists, money stuff, side-job planning — all in the same night sometimes. And I couldn’t really shut it off. Even if I told myself, “Okay, stop, just go sit down,” I’d think of one more thing I had to look up or write down.

It’s like I open my eyes and I’m already weighed down.\n\nAnd it’s strange because I’ll be sitting in my own condo — the place I worked really hard to get, and I used to feel proud of it — and instead of feeling comfortable or happy or anything, I just feel like I might cry. Sometimes I look around and think, “This should feel good, I should feel okay here,” but it just doesn’t. It feels kind of hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to it. So… yeah, sad and down, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been around for about five months, maybe a little more if I’m being honest. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly terrible, it was more like this fog started settling in and I kept thinking it would lift if I just got through the week, or caught up on things, or stopped overthinking.\n\nBut it’s been hanging around for months now — this depressed, guilty kind of feeling. And it definitely got worse after I bought the condo, which sounds backwards because that was supposed to be a good thing. I thought I’d feel proud or relieved, like I was moving forward, but instead it got heavier. So… about five months, with the worst of it more recently after the condo.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually been one of the weirder parts, because it’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… don’t feel anything toward them anymore.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It gave me something to look forward to, and I liked being around people there even if I wasn’t the best dancer or anything. But I just stopped going. At first I told myself I was just skipping one class, then it became another one, and then I kind of stopped even pretending I was going back.\n\nAnd painting too… I have my paints still sitting out on the table at home. They’ve just been there, like I left them expecting I’d pick them up again, but I’ll look at them and feel nothing. It’s almost like they belong to somebody else, which sounds strange, but that’s how it feels. Like I remember that I used to care about it, but I can’t really access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt significantly better over the past five months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were a few stretches where I felt really different, like almost weirdly better. Earlier this month especially, I remember feeling kind of lit up, like I had this energy or confidence that I hadn’t had in a while. Not just “a little less depressed,” but more like I suddenly felt unstoppable, like I could handle everything and maybe I’d been making too big a deal out of how bad I’d been feeling.\n\nBut it didn’t exactly feel peaceful, if that makes sense. It was like I was turned up too high. And then little things would set me off. My mom would ask me some normal question, like if I’d eaten or if I’d called someone back, and I’d snap at her like she was criticizing me. Or at work, my supervisor would ask a small follow-up question about something, nothing major, and I’d feel this flash of irritation, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I don’t think that would bother me that much.\n\nSo yes, I guess there have been times I felt significantly better, but not in a calm, steady way. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mood swings frequently over the past five months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.\n\nProbably about a month? But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mood swings frequently over the past five months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.\n\nProbably about a month? It started feeling noticeable once the mortgage became real, like after the condo stopped being this abstract “I’m buying a place” thing and became… this huge permanent responsibility. Since then it’s felt more like I swing between getting this burst of energy or confidence, and then dropping back into this heavy crash. Before that, I think I was mostly just down. But the noticeable ups and downs have been about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't need to sleep, or where you felt like you could go without sleep for an extended time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.\n\nNormally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see when you were feeling really up or charged?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where my thoughts are going really fast and I’m reading into things more than I normally would, but I haven’t heard voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nAnd I haven’t seen things either, not like seeing a person or something that other people couldn’t see. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.\n\nThen I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… not invincible exactly, like I didn’t think I could walk into traffic or something dramatic like that. But I did have this feeling like things couldn’t really fail if I just pushed hard enough.

Like I honestly started thinking I could buy the condo and pay it down really fast, even though when I’m calmer I can see that was probably not realistic for my income. And at the same time I was thinking, “I’ll just study for the CPA exams, I can pass them,” and then also, “I could run a bookkeeping business on the side.” It wasn’t just daydreaming, it felt like a plan, like of course I could do all of it.

So maybe not “nothing can go wrong” in those exact words, but failure just didn’t feel like an option in my head. I kind of brushed past the risks, like they were details I’d figure out later.

It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.\n\nThen I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. And then somehow that would turn into side-job ideas, like setting up bookkeeping work on the side, figuring out what I’d charge, what kind of clients I could get, making lists of business names or little plans.\n\nIt’s like after work, when normally I should be tired, I’d get this burst of energy and keep stacking things on. I kind of brushed past the risks, like they were details I’d figure out later.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever do things that might be considered reckless or impulsive, like spending a lot of money or acting out of character?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it reckless, exactly. I don’t think I’ve done anything really extreme or dangerous, like… I’m not going out and doing wild things completely unlike me.

The closest thing would probably be getting really caught up in money plans or future plans and feeling like I should act on them faster than I normally would. Like I might spend way too much time looking at costs or options and feel tempted to make a big decision, but I usually don’t fully go through with something huge. Or if I buy things, it’s more like things for a project or organizing or improving my place, not like I’m suddenly buying a car or gambling or something.

So… impulsive in my thinking, maybe? Like I get ahead of myself and everything feels urgent. But I don’t know if my actual behavior has crossed into what other people would call reckless. I guess I’d have to think about that more.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep has gotten really messed up lately. And other times I do fall asleep, but then I wake up way too early, like before I’m supposed to, and I just feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nIt’s been getting frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m starting the day normally anymore. I’m either lying there for hours waiting to fall asleep, or I’m awake too early and already exhausted. That’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s like I open my eyes and I’m already weighed down.\n\nAnd it’s strange because I’ll be sitting in my own condo — the place I worked really hard to get, and I used to feel proud of it — and instead of feeling comfortable or happy or anything, I just feel like I might cry. Sometimes I look around and think, “This should feel good, I should feel okay here,” but it just doesn’t. It feels kind of hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to it. So… yeah, sad and down, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been around for about five months, maybe a little more if I’m being honest. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly terrible, it was more like this fog started settling in and I kept thinking it would lift if I just got through the week, or caught up on things, or stopped overthinking.\n\nBut it’s been hanging around for months now — this depressed, guilty kind of feeling. And it definitely got worse after I bought the condo, which sounds backwards because that was supposed to be a good thing. I thought I’d feel proud or relieved, like I was moving forward, but instead it got heavier. That’s actually been one of the weirder parts, because it’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… don’t feel anything toward them anymore.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It gave me something to look forward to, and I liked being around people there even if I wasn’t the best dancer or anything. But I just stopped going. At first I told myself I was just skipping one class, then it became another one, and then I kind of stopped even pretending I was going back.\n\nAnd painting too… I have my paints still sitting out on the table at home. They’ve just been there, like I left them expecting I’d pick them up again, but I’ll look at them and feel nothing. It’s almost like they belong to somebody else, which sounds strange, but that’s how it feels. Like I remember that I used to care about it, but I can’t really access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt significantly better over the past five months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were a few stretches where I felt really different, like almost weirdly better. Earlier this month especially, I remember feeling kind of lit up, like I had this energy or confidence that I hadn’t had in a while. Not just “a little less depressed,” but more like I suddenly felt unstoppable, like I could handle everything and maybe I’d been making too big a deal out of how bad I’d been feeling.\n\nBut it didn’t exactly feel peaceful, if that makes sense. It was like I was turned up too high. And then little things would set me off. My mom would ask me some normal question, like if I’d eaten or if I’d called someone back, and I’d snap at her like she was criticizing me. Or at work, my supervisor would ask a small follow-up question about something, nothing major, and I’d feel this flash of irritation, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I don’t think that would bother me that much.\n\nSo yes, I guess there have been times I felt significantly better, but not in a calm, steady way. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mood swings frequently over the past five months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.\n\nProbably about a month? It started feeling noticeable once the mortgage became real, like after the condo stopped being this abstract “I’m buying a place” thing and became… this huge permanent responsibility. Since then it’s felt more like I swing between getting this burst of energy or confidence, and then dropping back into this heavy crash. Before that, I think I was mostly just down. But the noticeable ups and downs have been about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't need to sleep, or where you felt like you could go without sleep for an extended time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.\n\nNormally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see when you were feeling really up or charged?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. I don’t think so… at least not in a big, obvious way like quitting my job or suddenly ending a relationship. I haven’t had one of those moments where I just blow up my life and then later I’m like, “What did I do?”

I guess when I’m in that state, I can feel very ready to make decisions, like I’ll think, “I should just change everything,” or I’ll get this strong urge to start over or fix things immediately. But when it comes to actually doing something like walking out of work or cutting someone off completely, I don’t think I’ve really done that.

Sometimes I might say things more quickly or make plans that are kind of intense for me, like deciding I’m going to completely reorganize my schedule or take on some big personal project, but I usually don’t follow through with something irreversible. So… no, I don’t think I’ve quit a job or ended a relationship impulsively. If there are smaller things, I may not be recognizing them as impulsive, but nothing major comes to mind.

But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. Yeah… yes, I think so. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost, like, lit up from the inside. Not just in a good mood, but charged, like I had this extra engine running and everything felt more possible than usual.

And it wasn’t all happy, exactly. It would kind of flip into being really irritated. Like my mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was getting in my way. Or my supervisor would ask me to clarify something at work, and I’d feel this burst of annoyance, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I’d just answer and move on.

It lasted several days, yeah. It wasn’t just one afternoon where I had coffee or got good news. It was more like a few days in a row where I felt unstoppable, and then over little things I’d get sharp with people in a way that didn’t really fit the situation.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.0, F32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep has gotten really messed up lately. Like, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I should be tired, but then my mind just keeps going and going and I end up staying up half the night. And other times I do fall asleep, but then I wake up way too early, like before I’m supposed to, and I just feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nIt’s been getting frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m starting the day normally anymore. I’m either lying there for hours waiting to fall asleep, or I’m awake too early and already exhausted. That’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling already there, like before anything has even happened that day. It’s like I open my eyes and I’m already weighed down.\n\nAnd it’s strange because I’ll be sitting in my own condo — the place I worked really hard to get, and I used to feel proud of it — and instead of feeling comfortable or happy or anything, I just feel like I might cry. Sometimes I look around and think, “This should feel good, I should feel okay here,” but it just doesn’t. It feels kind of hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to it. So… yeah, sad and down, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been around for about five months, maybe a little more if I’m being honest. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly terrible, it was more like this fog started settling in and I kept thinking it would lift if I just got through the week, or caught up on things, or stopped overthinking.\n\nBut it’s been hanging around for months now — this depressed, guilty kind of feeling. And it definitely got worse after I bought the condo, which sounds backwards because that was supposed to be a good thing. I thought I’d feel proud or relieved, like I was moving forward, but instead it got heavier. So… about five months, with the worst of it more recently after the condo.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually been one of the weirder parts, because it’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… don’t feel anything toward them anymore.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It gave me something to look forward to, and I liked being around people there even if I wasn’t the best dancer or anything. But I just stopped going. At first I told myself I was just skipping one class, then it became another one, and then I kind of stopped even pretending I was going back.\n\nAnd painting too… I have my paints still sitting out on the table at home. They’ve just been there, like I left them expecting I’d pick them up again, but I’ll look at them and feel nothing. It’s almost like they belong to somebody else, which sounds strange, but that’s how it feels. Like I remember that I used to care about it, but I can’t really access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt significantly better over the past five months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were a few stretches where I felt really different, like almost weirdly better. Earlier this month especially, I remember feeling kind of lit up, like I had this energy or confidence that I hadn’t had in a while. Not just “a little less depressed,” but more like I suddenly felt unstoppable, like I could handle everything and maybe I’d been making too big a deal out of how bad I’d been feeling.\n\nBut it didn’t exactly feel peaceful, if that makes sense. It was like I was turned up too high. And then little things would set me off. My mom would ask me some normal question, like if I’d eaten or if I’d called someone back, and I’d snap at her like she was criticizing me. Or at work, my supervisor would ask a small follow-up question about something, nothing major, and I’d feel this flash of irritation, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I don’t think that would bother me that much.\n\nSo yes, I guess there have been times I felt significantly better, but not in a calm, steady way. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mood swings frequently over the past five months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.\n\nProbably about a month? It started feeling noticeable once the mortgage became real, like after the condo stopped being this abstract “I’m buying a place” thing and became… this huge permanent responsibility. Since then it’s felt more like I swing between getting this burst of energy or confidence, and then dropping back into this heavy crash. Before that, I think I was mostly just down. But the noticeable ups and downs have been about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't need to sleep, or where you felt like you could go without sleep for an extended time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.\n\nNormally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see when you were feeling really up or charged?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where my thoughts are going really fast and I’m reading into things more than I normally would, but I haven’t heard voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nAnd I haven’t seen things either, not like seeing a person or something that other people couldn’t see. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.\n\nThen I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. And then somehow that would turn into side-job ideas, like setting up bookkeeping work on the side, figuring out what I’d charge, what kind of clients I could get, making lists of business names or little plans.\n\nIt’s like after work, when normally I should be tired, I’d get this burst of energy and keep stacking things on. Cleaning, lists, money stuff, side-job planning — all in the same night sometimes. And I couldn’t really shut it off. Even if I told myself, “Okay, stop, just go sit down,” I’d think of one more thing I had to look up or write down.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you feel like you're invincible or that nothing can go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not invincible exactly, like I didn’t think I could walk into traffic or something dramatic like that. But I did have this feeling like things couldn’t really fail if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nLike I honestly started thinking I could buy the condo and pay it down really fast, even though when I’m calmer I can see that was probably not realistic for my income. And at the same time I was thinking, “I’ll just study for the CPA exams, I can pass them,” and then also, “I could run a bookkeeping business on the side.” It wasn’t just daydreaming, it felt like a plan, like of course I could do all of it.\n\nSo maybe not “nothing can go wrong” in those exact words, but failure just didn’t feel like an option in my head. I kind of brushed past the risks, like they were details I’d figure out later.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever do things that might be considered reckless or impulsive, like spending a lot of money or acting out of character?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it reckless, exactly. I don’t think I’ve done anything really extreme or dangerous, like… I’m not going out and doing wild things completely unlike me.\n\nThe closest thing would probably be getting really caught up in money plans or future plans and feeling like I should act on them faster than I normally would. Like I might spend way too much time looking at costs or options and feel tempted to make a big decision, but I usually don’t fully go through with something huge. Or if I buy things, it’s more like things for a project or organizing or improving my place, not like I’m suddenly buying a car or gambling or something.\n\nSo… impulsive in my thinking, maybe? Like I get ahead of myself and everything feels urgent. But I don’t know if my actual behavior has crossed into what other people would call reckless. I guess I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Have you done anything impulsive that doesn't involve money, like quitting a job, ending a relationship, or doing something spontaneous that's out of character for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in a big, obvious way like quitting my job or suddenly ending a relationship. I haven’t had one of those moments where I just blow up my life and then later I’m like, “What did I do?”\n\nI guess when I’m in that state, I can feel very ready to make decisions, like I’ll think, “I should just change everything,” or I’ll get this strong urge to start over or fix things immediately. But when it comes to actually doing something like walking out of work or cutting someone off completely, I don’t think I’ve really done that.\n\nSometimes I might say things more quickly or make plans that are kind of intense for me, like deciding I’m going to completely reorganize my schedule or take on some big personal project, but I usually don’t follow through with something irreversible. So… no, I don’t think I’ve quit a job or ended a relationship impulsively. If there are smaller things, I may not be recognizing them as impulsive, but nothing major comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost, like, lit up from the inside. Not just in a good mood, but charged, like I had this extra engine running and everything felt more possible than usual.\n\nAnd it wasn’t all happy, exactly. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… I think that’s what I was trying to describe. Earlier this month, there were several days where I felt really elevated for no clear reason. Like nothing major had happened that would explain it — I hadn’t gotten some amazing news or anything — but I just felt kind of lit up, like I had this sense that I could handle everything and that I was almost unstoppable.

It wasn’t just “I’m having a nice day,” either. It felt bigger than that, like my mood was turned up too high. And at first it almost felt good, because I was more confident and energized in my head, but then it would switch really quickly into irritation. Like my mom would ask me a normal question, something that shouldn’t have bothered me at all, and I’d snap at her. Or my supervisor would ask me to clarify something, and I’d feel this immediate annoyance, like they were getting in my way or questioning me, even though normally I’d just answer.

So yes, it did feel elevated for several days, and I don’t think there was an obvious reason for it. It kind of came on like this charged feeling, and then I was more reactive than usual.

If there are smaller things, I may not be recognizing them as impulsive, but nothing major comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost, like, lit up from the inside. Not just in a good mood, but charged, like I had this extra engine running and everything felt more possible than usual.\n\nAnd it wasn’t all happy, exactly. It would kind of flip into being really irritated. Like my mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was getting in my way. Or my supervisor would ask me to clarify something at work, and I’d feel this burst of annoyance, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I’d just answer and move on.\n\nIt lasted several days, yeah. It wasn’t just one afternoon where I had coffee or got good news. It was more like a few days in a row where I felt unstoppable, and then over little things I’d get sharp with people in a way that didn’t really fit the situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row, without any apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I was trying to describe. Earlier this month, there were several days where I felt really elevated for no clear reason. Like nothing major had happened that would explain it — I hadn’t gotten some amazing news or anything — but I just felt kind of lit up, like I had this sense that I could handle everything and that I was almost unstoppable.\n\nIt wasn’t just “I’m having a nice day,” either. It felt bigger than that, like my mood was turned up too high. And at first it almost felt good, because I was more confident and energized in my head, but then it would switch really quickly into irritation. Like my mom would ask me a normal question, something that shouldn’t have bothered me at all, and I’d snap at her. Or my supervisor would ask me to clarify something, and I’d feel this immediate annoyance, like they were getting in my way or questioning me, even though normally I’d just answer.\n\nSo yes, it did feel elevated for several days, and I don’t think there was an obvious reason for it. It kind of came on like this charged feeling, and then I was more reactive than usual.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think anything in particular triggers these mood swings of feeling really up or charged, and then crashing back down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"The really obvious cycling between that charged-up stretch and this crash has been going on for about a month, since the mortgage became real.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think the biggest thing I can point to is the mortgage. Like, once that became real — not just an idea or something we were talking about in the future, but actually real — that’s when I started noticing the more obvious cycling.

It’s been about a month, I’d say. Before that, I’m sure I had moods, obviously, but this more noticeable pattern of feeling really charged up and then coming back down hard… that seems like it started around then. It’s like my brain latched onto it as this huge life thing, and since then I’ve been going up and down in a way that feels much more obvious to me.

So I don’t know if it’s the only trigger, but that’s the main one I can identify. The mortgage becoming real feels like the point where this all got louder.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.0, F31.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep has gotten really messed up lately. Like, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I should be tired, but then my mind just keeps going and going and I end up staying up half the night. And other times I do fall asleep, but then I wake up way too early, like before I’m supposed to, and I just feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nIt’s been getting frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m starting the day normally anymore. I’m either lying there for hours waiting to fall asleep, or I’m awake too early and already exhausted. That’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling already there, like before anything has even happened that day. It’s like I open my eyes and I’m already weighed down.\n\nAnd it’s strange because I’ll be sitting in my own condo — the place I worked really hard to get, and I used to feel proud of it — and instead of feeling comfortable or happy or anything, I just feel like I might cry. Sometimes I look around and think, “This should feel good, I should feel okay here,” but it just doesn’t. It feels kind of hollow, like I’m there but not really connected to it. So… yeah, sad and down, definitely.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been around for about five months, maybe a little more if I’m being honest. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly terrible, it was more like this fog started settling in and I kept thinking it would lift if I just got through the week, or caught up on things, or stopped overthinking.\n\nBut it’s been hanging around for months now — this depressed, guilty kind of feeling. And it definitely got worse after I bought the condo, which sounds backwards because that was supposed to be a good thing. I thought I’d feel proud or relieved, like I was moving forward, but instead it got heavier. So… about five months, with the worst of it more recently after the condo.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s actually been one of the weirder parts, because it’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… don’t feel anything toward them anymore.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It gave me something to look forward to, and I liked being around people there even if I wasn’t the best dancer or anything. But I just stopped going. At first I told myself I was just skipping one class, then it became another one, and then I kind of stopped even pretending I was going back.\n\nAnd painting too… I have my paints still sitting out on the table at home. They’ve just been there, like I left them expecting I’d pick them up again, but I’ll look at them and feel nothing. It’s almost like they belong to somebody else, which sounds strange, but that’s how it feels. Like I remember that I used to care about it, but I can’t really access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt significantly better over the past five months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were a few stretches where I felt really different, like almost weirdly better. Earlier this month especially, I remember feeling kind of lit up, like I had this energy or confidence that I hadn’t had in a while. Not just “a little less depressed,” but more like I suddenly felt unstoppable, like I could handle everything and maybe I’d been making too big a deal out of how bad I’d been feeling.\n\nBut it didn’t exactly feel peaceful, if that makes sense. It was like I was turned up too high. And then little things would set me off. My mom would ask me some normal question, like if I’d eaten or if I’d called someone back, and I’d snap at her like she was criticizing me. Or at work, my supervisor would ask a small follow-up question about something, nothing major, and I’d feel this flash of irritation, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I don’t think that would bother me that much.\n\nSo yes, I guess there have been times I felt significantly better, but not in a calm, steady way. More like suddenly really up and then kind of sharp with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these mood swings frequently over the past five months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been the whole five months in that really obvious back-and-forth way. The low feeling has been there for months, but the actual cycling — like being really charged up and then crashing down again — that’s been much more recent.\n\nProbably about a month? It started feeling noticeable once the mortgage became real, like after the condo stopped being this abstract “I’m buying a place” thing and became… this huge permanent responsibility. Since then it’s felt more like I swing between getting this burst of energy or confidence, and then dropping back into this heavy crash. Before that, I think I was mostly just down. But the noticeable ups and downs have been about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't need to sleep, or where you felt like you could go without sleep for an extended time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that has happened. Not like I was trying to pull an all-nighter or anything, but there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and then I’d wake up feeling strangely charged.\n\nNormally if I sleep that little, I’m useless the next day — like I need coffee immediately and I’m dragging myself around. But during those nights, I’d get up and feel like I didn’t even need coffee, like my body was already running on something. It felt almost confusing, because part of me knew, “You barely slept, you should be exhausted,” but I just wasn’t. I’d be awake and alert really quickly, like I was ready to start doing things.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever feel like you're capable of doing anything or that you're more important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s embarrassing to say out loud because now it sounds kind of unrealistic, but when I’m in that charged-up place, I really do start thinking like I can handle anything if I just push hard enough.\n\nLike with the condo, I wasn’t just thinking, “Okay, I can manage the payments.” I was thinking I could somehow pay it down really fast, like way faster than would probably make sense on my income. And then I’d start stacking other things on top of that in my head, like, “I should just study for the CPA exams, I could pass them, and I could start a little bookkeeping business on the side too.” It all felt completely possible at the time, like failure wasn’t even part of the equation.\n\nIt wasn’t like I thought I was famous or anything like that, but I definitely felt more capable than usual. More… important, maybe, or like I was finally seeing how much I could do and everyone else was just underestimating me. Then later, when I’m not in that mood, I look at it and think, “That was a lot. Where did that confidence even come from?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see when you were feeling really up or charged?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where my thoughts are going really fast and I’m reading into things more than I normally would, but I haven’t heard voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nAnd I haven’t seen things either, not like seeing a person or something that other people couldn’t see. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.\n\nThen I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. And then somehow that would turn into side-job ideas, like setting up bookkeeping work on the side, figuring out what I’d charge, what kind of clients I could get, making lists of business names or little plans.\n\nIt’s like after work, when normally I should be tired, I’d get this burst of energy and keep stacking things on. Cleaning, lists, money stuff, side-job planning — all in the same night sometimes. And I couldn’t really shut it off. Even if I told myself, “Okay, stop, just go sit down,” I’d think of one more thing I had to look up or write down.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you feel like you're invincible or that nothing can go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not invincible exactly, like I didn’t think I could walk into traffic or something dramatic like that. But I did have this feeling like things couldn’t really fail if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nLike I honestly started thinking I could buy the condo and pay it down really fast, even though when I’m calmer I can see that was probably not realistic for my income. And at the same time I was thinking, “I’ll just study for the CPA exams, I can pass them,” and then also, “I could run a bookkeeping business on the side.” It wasn’t just daydreaming, it felt like a plan, like of course I could do all of it.\n\nSo maybe not “nothing can go wrong” in those exact words, but failure just didn’t feel like an option in my head. I kind of brushed past the risks, like they were details I’d figure out later.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever do things that might be considered reckless or impulsive, like spending a lot of money or acting out of character?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it reckless, exactly. I don’t think I’ve done anything really extreme or dangerous, like… I’m not going out and doing wild things completely unlike me.\n\nThe closest thing would probably be getting really caught up in money plans or future plans and feeling like I should act on them faster than I normally would. Like I might spend way too much time looking at costs or options and feel tempted to make a big decision, but I usually don’t fully go through with something huge. Or if I buy things, it’s more like things for a project or organizing or improving my place, not like I’m suddenly buying a car or gambling or something.\n\nSo… impulsive in my thinking, maybe? Like I get ahead of myself and everything feels urgent. But I don’t know if my actual behavior has crossed into what other people would call reckless. I guess I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Have you done anything impulsive that doesn't involve money, like quitting a job, ending a relationship, or doing something spontaneous that's out of character for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in a big, obvious way like quitting my job or suddenly ending a relationship. I haven’t had one of those moments where I just blow up my life and then later I’m like, “What did I do?”\n\nI guess when I’m in that state, I can feel very ready to make decisions, like I’ll think, “I should just change everything,” or I’ll get this strong urge to start over or fix things immediately. But when it comes to actually doing something like walking out of work or cutting someone off completely, I don’t think I’ve really done that.\n\nSometimes I might say things more quickly or make plans that are kind of intense for me, like deciding I’m going to completely reorganize my schedule or take on some big personal project, but I usually don’t follow through with something irreversible. So… no, I don’t think I’ve quit a job or ended a relationship impulsively. If there are smaller things, I may not be recognizing them as impulsive, but nothing major comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Or my supervisor would ask me to clarify something at work, and I’d feel this burst of annoyance, like, “Why are you questioning me?” even though normally I’d just answer and move on.\n\nIt lasted several days, yeah. It wasn’t just one afternoon where I had coffee or got good news. It was more like a few days in a row where I felt unstoppable, and then over little things I’d get sharp with people in a way that didn’t really fit the situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row, without any apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I was trying to describe. Sometimes when I’m really keyed up I might feel more alert to sounds or notice stuff more intensely, like every little noise stands out, but I don’t think I’ve had anything where I’d say I was hearing or seeing something that wasn’t real.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you find yourself taking on more projects or activities than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly afterward, because in the moment it feels productive, like I’m just finally getting my life together.\n\nI’ll come home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’ll start cleaning like the whole place needs to be fixed right then. Not just regular cleaning either — I mean pulling things out of cabinets, reorganizing stuff, making piles, wiping down things I usually ignore. And while I’m doing that, I’ll be making renovation lists in my head or on my phone, like what I’d change in the kitchen, what flooring would cost, what I could paint myself, what I’d need a contractor for.\n\nThen I’d switch over to checking interest rates or mortgage calculators, just clicking around and comparing numbers like it was urgent. And then somehow that would turn into side-job ideas, like setting up bookkeeping work on the side, figuring out what I’d charge, what kind of clients I could get, making lists of business names or little plans.\n\nIt’s like after work, when normally I should be tired, I’d get this burst of energy and keep stacking things on. Cleaning, lists, money stuff, side-job planning — all in the same night sometimes. And I couldn’t really shut it off. Even if I told myself, “Okay, stop, just go sit down,” I’d think of one more thing I had to look up or write down.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you feel like you're invincible or that nothing can go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not invincible exactly, like I didn’t think I could walk into traffic or something dramatic like that. But I did have this feeling like things couldn’t really fail if I just pushed hard enough.\n\nLike I honestly started thinking I could buy the condo and pay it down really fast, even though when I’m calmer I can see that was probably not realistic for my income. And at the same time I was thinking, “I’ll just study for the CPA exams, I can pass them,” and then also, “I could run a bookkeeping business on the side.” It wasn’t just daydreaming, it felt like a plan, like of course I could do all of it.\n\nSo maybe not “nothing can go wrong” in those exact words, but failure just didn’t feel like an option in my head. I kind of brushed past the risks, like they were details I’d figure out later.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling really up or charged, do you ever do things that might be considered reckless or impulsive, like spending a lot of money or acting out of character?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it reckless, exactly. Like I get ahead of myself and everything feels urgent. But I don’t know if my actual behavior has crossed into what other people would call reckless. I haven’t had one of those moments where I just blow up my life and then later I’m like, “What did I do?”\n\nI guess when I’m in that state, I can feel very ready to make decisions, like I’ll think, “I should just change everything,” or I’ll get this strong urge to start over or fix things immediately. But when it comes to actually doing something like walking out of work or cutting someone off completely, I don’t think I’ve really done that.\n\nSometimes I might say things more quickly or make plans that are kind of intense for me, like deciding I’m going to completely reorganize my schedule or take on some big personal project, but I usually don’t follow through with something irreversible. Yes. Earlier this month, there was definitely a stretch like that — at least several days in a row — where I felt… I don’t know, almost lit up. Like I had this extra push in me and I could do anything, or handle everything, and I remember thinking I felt kind of unstoppable.

Or at work, if my supervisor asked me to clarify something, I’d feel immediately annoyed, like they were criticizing me or slowing me down, even though looking back they were just asking a basic question.

So yes, it did last for several days, and it felt noticeably different from my normal mood.
